Effects of therapeutic patient education interventions on diabetes self-management outcomes in the Eastern Mediterranean Region: a systematic review
Authors: Dayana El Chaar, Hicham El Berri, Matilda Byström, Eiman Hag, Lamia Mahmoud, Asmus Hammerich, Zoltan Pataky, Jorge C. Correia, Cosette Fakih El Khoury
Published: 2025

ABSTRACT
BackgroundTherapeutic patient education (TPE) is a structured, person-centered educational process designed to empower individuals living with non-communicable diseases, such as diabetes mellitus. Educational interventions addressing diabetes in the Eastern Mediterranean Region (EMR) are isolated , and their effectiveness is not well reported. Therefore, this systematic review evaluates the impact of TPE interventions on diabetes self-management outcomes in the EMR.MethodsWe searched six databases (MEDLINE OVID, PubMed, Web of Science, Scopus, CINAHL, and ScienceDirect) for relevant articles. Articles were included if they reflected on TPE initiatives addressing diabetes, conducted in the EMR, delivered by healthcare professionals or multidisciplinary healthcare teams, and utilized TPE components and/or constructs. Data extraction included self-management outcomes of diabetes mellitus and the country of intervention. Effect sizes were computed for all outcomes.Results24 articles, including TPE constructs (knowledge, skills, and confidence) or components (goal setting, action planning, shared decision-making), were included in the analysis. Most articles were randomized controlled trials (n = 14). Workshops and educational sessions in group settings were the most common modality of TPE interventions (n = 18). Results were reported based on self-management outcomes: [1] self-care [2], anthropometric and lifestyle, and [3] biological outcomes. The combination of knowledge and confidence (n = 8), and goal setting (n = 8) were the most reported combinations of the patient competency profile and TPE components, respectively. Most studies (n = 18) reported on self-care outcomes, such as general or specific diet adherence, blood glucose monitoring, foot care, and medication adherence, with effect sizes ranging from small to large. 16 articles also reported on biological measures (glycemic, lipid profile, and blood pressure measurements), 11 on anthropometric outcomes (body mass index-BMI, weight, and waist circumference), 4 on dietary outcomes, and 4 on physical activity outcomes. Mixed effect sizes were observed for anthropometric and biological outcomes, with most studies revealing small effect sizes for changes in BMI, weight, and blood lipids. In contrast, large and significant effect sizes for waist circumference, HbA1c, and fasting blood glucose were noted.ConclusionDespite varying effect sizes for most outcomes, TPE interventions in the EMR showed significant improvements in several diabetes self-management outcomes, including waist circumference, HbA1c, and fasting blood glucose. TPE components and constructs were more prevalent with significant improvements and considerable effect in self-care and biological outcomes, particularly when the TPE constructs were utilized in combinations of two or more.PROSPERO, registration number CRD42024601438.Supplementary InformationThe online version contains supplementary material available at 10.1186/s12889-025-24721-w.

Introduction

The global diabetes epidemic has created a significant health and economic concern, particularly in the Eastern Mediterranean region (EMR). In 2024, the prevalence of type 2 diabetes in the Middle East and North Africa (MENA) region was 17.6% among adults aged 20–79 years [1, 2]. This is still expected to rise in the EMR to the second highest around the globe by 2045 [3–5]. Multiple challenges in the EMR have hindered efforts to control the diabetes epidemic [5]. Standard approaches to diabetes treatment, such as the use of insulin and oral glucose-lowering medications, have been in place for decades; however, many individuals demonstrate low adherence to treatment on a long-term basis or still lack appropriate access to these medications due to various socioeconomic and healthcare-related barriers. In light of this public health concern, it remains essential to seek effective and innovative management and treatment strategies for diabetes to improve concordance to treatment and reduce the burden of the disease on the populations and governments [5].

Educational interventions have been considered a key element in the treatment and management of individuals with diabetes [6]. However, traditional educational interventions rely on passive learning, where healthcare providers direct the treatment process while excluding individuals from actively managing their condition or making decisions regarding their treatment plans [7]. On the other hand, therapeutic patient education (TPE) is a structured, person-centered educational process designed to empower individuals living with chronic conditions, such as diabetes, to self-manage their health [6]. TPE aims to support the self-management of chronic diseases by managing [1] the medical aspect of the disease [2], the life roles of individuals, and [3] the psychological and emotional consequences of the condition [6]. TPE also aims to support the prevention of diabetes-related complications that could occur over the individual’s lifetime. This is particularly achieved through a continuous process that does not stop after the first educational encounter and evolves over time, adapting to the changing needs and circumstances of the individual. This non-traditional educational approach is typically delivered by trained healthcare providers of various expertise and includes self-management support interventions tailored to the individual’s condition and needs [6].

In 2023, the World Health Organization (WHO) published an introductory guide on TPE. In addition to introducing TPE, this guide also emphasized the TPE competency framework and the key components of TPE [6]. The TPE competency framework, which consists of knowledge, skill, and confidence, is the cornerstone that supports TPE interventions and ensures that they lead to the desired behavioral change and, thus, the ability of individuals to self-manage their condition. Additional key components of the TPE wheel include [1] goal-setting [2], action planning, and [3] shared decision-making, which are also crucial elements that support behavior change in individuals with non-communicable diseases (NCDs) [6].

With the increasing prevalence of diabetes in the EMR and its complications [5] it is essential to assess the effectiveness of TPE interventions on diabetes outcomes. There is a substantial body of literature that reports on the use of multiple educational interventions in the EMR to manage diabetes by assessing various outcomes. However, to our knowledge, the interventions are fragmented, and the educational approaches used are not well defined. Additionally, the influence of the TPE competency framework and TPE components in various educational interventions on diabetes outcomes is underreported, with limited evidence currently available in the literature. Therefore, the objective of this study is to primarily explore and assess the impact of TPE interventions on diabetes self-management outcomes, including self-care, biological, lifestyle, and anthropometric outcomes in the EMR. We aim to report on the available outcomes by synthesizing the latest publications that address the impact of TPE on diabetes outcomes in the EMR. Moreover, our review seeks to contribute to the effectiveness of tailored TPE interventions for diabetes management in the EMR.

Methodology

Study protocol

This systematic review aims to identify the effects of TPE interventions on diabetes self-management outcomes in the EMR (EMR countries: Afghanistan, Bahrain, Djibouti, Egypt, Iran, Islamic Republic of Iraq, Jordan, Lebanon, Libya, Morocco, Occupied Palestinian Territory, Oman, Pakistan, Palestine, Qatar, Saudi Arabia, Somalia, Sudan, Syrian Arab Republic, Tunisia, United Arab Emirates, Yemen) [8]. The definition of TPE adopted is based on the recent introductory guide published by the WHO; this guide defines TPE as a “structured person-centered learning process that supports individuals living with chronic conditions to self-manage their own health by drawing on their own resources, supported by their carers and families” [6].

This systematic review is part of a broader systematic review with a larger scope, for which the protocol was registered in the International Prospective Register of Systematic Reviews (PROSPERO, registration number CRD42024601438, registered on 29/10/2024). The amended scope of the review covered TPE practices across all NCDs, including diabetes, cardiovascular diseases, hypertension, overweight and obesity, and other NCDs in the EMR. However, due to the exhaustive results, articles only focusing on TPE interventions for the self-management of individuals living with diabetes were included in this article.

Search strategy

Multiple search terms were employed to identify publications studying TPE in the EMR. The following concepts were used in the search strategy: “Therapeutic Patient Education”, “Non-communicable Diseases”, and “Eastern Mediterranean Region”. The search included both indexed terminology and keywords. The Boolean operator < AND > was used to ensure the inclusion of all concepts, and < OR > to expand the search to include keywords with similar meanings. The full search strategy adapted for each database is available as an additional file (see Additional File 1).

The search was undertaken using major academic databases, including MEDLINE OVID, PubMed, Web of Science, Scopus, CINAHL, and ScienceDirect, to identify relevant studies to TPE interventions in the EMR. The review follows the reporting guidelines recommended by the PRISMA (Preferred Reporting Items for Systematic Reviews and Meta-Analyses) guidelines for reporting systematic reviews (Fig. 2) [9]. A completed PRISMA checklist is provided as an additional file (see Additional File 2).

We searched Google and Google Scholar to identify articles, reports, or guidelines that discuss person-centered therapeutic education initiatives in the selected countries of the EMR. Relevant keywords such as “therapeutic patient education”, “health education”, “patient education”, and “chronic disease” were used.

Grouping and eligibility criteria

Articles that reflect TPE initiatives conducted in the EMR countries that were included focused on educational interventions or programs that are person-centered and that are delivered by a healthcare professional or a multidisciplinary healthcare team.

Only studies that were conducted in EMR countries were included in the review. Qualitative, quantitative, and mixed-methods studies written in English, French, or Arabic and published between 2000 and 2024 were included. The included studies addressed at least two components of the TPE competency framework (knowledge, skills, and confidence) and used constructs from the TPE Wheel, such as goal setting, action planning, and shared decision-making, as proposed by the WHO TPE guide [6].

Articles that did not meet the inclusion criteria were excluded. Articles of TPE initiatives conducted outside EMR counties, or educational interventions broadly addressing individuals with NCDs, or initiatives that do not include any TPE components, were also excluded. Articles that were published before 2000 were not included in the results. Study designs included randomized controlled trials (RCTs), quasi-experimental studies, and pre-posttest studies, which were analyzed separately to account for differences in methodological rigor. Given the nature of educational intervention in studies, we included different interventional study designs to ensure a comprehensive assessment of the effectiveness of TPE-based interventions across diverse research contexts.

We employed the PICO (Population, Intervention, Comparison, and Outcome) framework to guide the studies’ systematic selection and evaluation. Details on the research question and each criterion of the PICO framework are presented in the additional material (see Additional File 3) [10].

Data extraction and analysis

Articles retrieved from the search strategy were imported into EndNote and reviewed. Articles were screened for eligibility by two reviewers (D.C. and T.D.); in case of disagreements, the senior investigator was consulted to reach a final resolution. Using a data extraction form, information on the interventions and their modalities was extracted, including participant characteristics, country of intervention, description of the intervention, results, and TPE constructs of the interventions. Means, standard deviations, mean differences, and changes in proportions and frequencies of self-management or adherence were reported. Effect sizes were computed using Cohen’s d or Cohen’s h (for categorical data such as changes in proportions and frequencies). Interpretation of the effect size followed standard guidelines: small (0.2), medium (0.5), and large (0.8) effect sizes [11]. Heterogeneity in study designs, outcomes reported, and metrics used did not allow for the performance of a meta-analysis; accordingly, vote counting was reported based on the direction of the effect [12].

Data synthesis

Self-care outcomes were grouped into primary outcomes (self-care behaviors like medication adherence, blood sugar monitoring, foot care, and adherence to general or specific diet and physical activity) and secondary outcomes [1] biological measures such as glycated hemoglobin (HbA1c), blood glucose, lipid profile, blood pressure; [2] anthropometric measures such as weight, body mass index (BMI), and waist circumference; and [3] lifestyle changes like diet and physical activity].

The rationale used for this grouping is illustrated by the logic model based on Anderson (Fig. 1) [13] as proposed by the reporting guidelines of Synthesis without meta-analysis (SWiM) in systematic reviews [12]. Fig. 1Logic model used in the systematic review for TPE interventions

Logic model used in the systematic review for TPE interventions

Risk of bias assessment

The two different versions of the RoB2, Version 2 of the Cochrane risk-of-bias tool for randomized trials, were used for individual parallel RCT or crossover trials [14]. While the JBI (Joanna Briggs Institute) critical appraisal tool was employed to assess the risk of bias in quasi-experimental studies, pre-post studies, and other experimental studies reported in our results [15].

Results

Included articles: segregated by the country of publication and study design

The search strategy resulted in a total of 11,078 articles. After careful revisions of the articles and exclusions, 48 articles included educational interventions on diabetes self-management, of which only 24 included constructs from the competency framework (knowledge, skills, and confidence) or components that reflect on TPE approaches (goal setting, action planning, shared decision-making) (Fig. 2) [16, 17]. It is important to acknowledge that the results of this systematic review revealed great disparities between publication outputs regarding TPE interventions across the EMR countries. Iran had the highest TPE publications among the EMR countries (n = 15) [18–32]; while other countries from the region had comparatively lower contributions, such as Egypt (n = 3) [33–35], Saudi Arabia (n = 3) [36–38], United Arab Emirates (n = 2) [16, 17], and Qatar (n = 1) [39].Fig. 2PRISMA flow diagram

PRISMA flow diagram

Results from our review also showed a variety in the study design of the included articles. Most of the studies were RCTs with a parallel design (n = 13) [16, 17, 19–22, 24–26, 28, 29, 31, 39] with only 1 study with a crossover design [35]. The rest of the articles were either quasi-experimental studies (n = 6) [18, 23, 27, 32–34], pre-test and post-test studies (n = 2) [36, 38], or experimental studies that lack a control group (n = 2) [30, 37].

Characteristics of the interventions

The most common modalities for delivering TPE interventions were workshops and educational sessions provided in group settings for individuals with diabetes (n = 18) (Fig. 3, a) [17–20, 22–24, 26–29, 31, 32, 35–39]. The education delivery, when specified, was provided by multidisciplinary teams (n = 3) [20, 24, 32], nurses (n = 3) [19, 34, 38], dietitians (n = 3) [16, 17, 31], or diabetes/health educators (n = 3) [24, 29, 39] (Fig. 3,b). Interventions that involved pharmacists [35], facilitators [22], and academics [30] were less common than interventions delivered by other healthcare practitioners. This collaborative, multidisciplinary approach emphasizes the importance of a holistic healthcare team in imparting diabetes-related knowledge, skills, and/or confidence to individuals with diabetes within the EMR.

Fig. 3a Modality of Educational Interventions Delivered to Individuals with Diabetes Mellitus in the Eastern Mediterranean Region.b Educators Delivering the Educational Interventions to Individuals with Diabetes Mellitus in the Eastern Mediterranean Region

a Modality of Educational Interventions Delivered to Individuals with Diabetes Mellitus in the Eastern Mediterranean Region.b Educators Delivering the Educational Interventions to Individuals with Diabetes Mellitus in the Eastern Mediterranean Region

Characteristics of the populations in the included articles

The populations included in the studies were predominantly adults diagnosed with type 2 diabetes mellitus (T2DM), ranging from young adults to older adults. Studies reported varying sample sizes ranging from 65 to 430 participants. Few articles focused on particular groups, such as individuals with diabetes and living in rural areas [29], older adults [25], or women with gestational diabetes [36]. The majority of the participants were recruited from their outpatient diabetes clinics.

Components of TPE and the patient competency framework in the included articles

Articles were also reviewed to determine the use of TPE components, such as goal setting, action planning, and shared decision-making, and to address the patient competency framework elements, namely knowledge, skills, and confidence [6].

Results revealed that goal setting was the component most frequently used across the included TPE interventions (n = 8) (Fig. 4a) [16, 17, 20, 23, 24, 31, 33, 39]. Action planning (n = 5) [27, 31–33, 39] and decision-making (n = 3) [21, 29, 31], along with their combinations [31], were reported less often. Only one article utilized all three components of TPE in its educational intervention (Fig. 4, a) [31]. Details on the components and constructs of TPE in each included article are presented in the additional material (see Additional File 4).

Fig. 4a Use of Therapeutic Patient Education Components in Diabetes Educational Interventions in the Eastern Mediterranean Region. b Use of Therapeutic Patient Education Elements in Diabetes Educational Interventions in the Eastern Mediterranean Region

a Use of Therapeutic Patient Education Components in Diabetes Educational Interventions in the Eastern Mediterranean Region. b Use of Therapeutic Patient Education Elements in Diabetes Educational Interventions in the Eastern Mediterranean Region

A total of 20 articles addressed at least two elements of the patient competency framework (Fig. 4,b) [18–30, 33–39]. Knowledge and confidence/self-efficacy were the most reported combinations of the patient competency profile in the included articles (n = 8) [19, 20, 22, 27, 30, 32, 35, 39]. On the other hand, the combination of that framework, which includes skill and confidence/self-efficacy, was the least utilized of the elements of the patient competency profile (n = 2) (Fig. 4,b) [24, 25].

Behavioral theories used in the formulation of the educational interventions of diabetes Self-Management

The use of theory-based interventions and frameworks was common across the reviewed studies. Behavioral theories were also used in some of the included studies (n = 9). The Preceed-Proceed Model [18, 26], the Health Belief Model [22, 27], Social Cognitive Theory [23, 25], Pender’s Health Promotion Model [32], and Cognitive Behavioral Theory [16, 19] were among the reported behavioral theories.

Self-care outcomes: primary outcome of diabetes Self-Management

A total of 18 studies reported self-care outcomes, of which 9 were RCTs [20–22, 24, 25, 29, 31, 35, 37], 5 were quasi experimental [18, 23, 27, 32–34], 2 were experimental studies [26, 30], and 1 was a pre-post design [36] (Table 1). The Summary of Diabetes Self-Care Activities (SDSCA) questionnaire was the most utilized questionnaire to assess the changes in self-care (n = 5) [18–21, 33]. Adherence to dietary practices, physical activity, medication, blood glucose monitoring, smoking, eye care, and foot care was also reported. Overall, all studies demonstrated significant improvements in at least one of the self-care behaviors following an educational intervention with TPE components or patient competency profile elements. Five studies reported on adherence to diets, general or specific diets, with two reporting a small effect size [20, 31] with non-significant results for adherence to a general [20] and low-fat diet [20, 31], two reporting a medium effect size with significant change [33, 34], and only one [30] reporting a large effect size and significant change in the intervention group. For instance, Shakibazadeh et al. revealed a small effect size when adhering to a general diet after the 8 weeks of the educational intervention (Cohen’s d = 0.46, p = 0.07), and a specific diet (Cohen’s d = 0.35, p < 0.001) [20]. On the other hand, Rahbar et al. showed a significant and large effect size for adherence to dietary plans in the intervention group after the implementation of the educational intervention (Cohen’s d = −3.40, p < 0.001) [30]. Similarly, 6 articles reported on the frequency of exercising as part of the self-care assessment [20, 21, 30, 31, 33, 34] with effect sizes ranging from small to large [20, 21, 30, 31, 33, 34]. Three articles showed a large effect size and significant changes in adherence to and frequency of exercising as part of the self-care model [17, 21, 34], one study reported a medium and significant effect size [20], while two studies showed small effect sizes [31, 33], with one reporting insignificant results between web-based and counseling groups after the educational intervention [33]. For instance, Shakibazadeh et al. observed a medium effect size (Cohen’s d = 0.54, p = 0.01) [20], while Rahbar et al. reported a large effect size (Cohen’s d = −3.47, p < 0.001) [30, 33]. Articles reporting on the frequency of blood glucose monitoring showed a large effect size for two studies [21, 33], one medium [34] and one small effect size [37]. All articles reporting on blood glucose monitoring showed a significant change in blood glucose testing [20, 21, 30, 34, 37]. Adherence to medications was significantly reported in five studies of this review, and an equal number of articles showed small (n = 2) [32, 34] and large effect sizes (n = 2) [21, 30], with only one showing a medium effect size [37]. For example, Zeidi et al. (2020) showed significant increases in medication adherence and blood glucose testing with a large effect size (Cohen’s d = 5.56 and Cohen’s d = 2.61, p < 0.001, respectively) [21]. Five articles that reported foot care management showed a significant improvement in foot care testing with varied effect sizes ranging from small to large effect sizes [20, 30, 33, 34, 37]. The majority of the articles assessing foot care showed a large effect size of the intervention [20, 21, 30], with only two articles showing low and medium effect sizes. Diabetes self-management was also significantly reported in five studies that revealed a large effect size of the intervention [22–24, 26, 35].

Self-care outcomes in the included articles

I1: educational intervention

C (n = 140): usual 30-min lecture-based non-interactive educational sessions; educational materials included pamphlets with limited topics on the definition of the diabetes, foot-care, diet and exercise

Eight 2½-h educational workshops offered over a 4-week period and followed by two ‘booster’ sessions, each 2 weeks apart, 8 week follow up

Education based on 7 areas of diabetes self-management including (i) healthy eating, (ii) being active, (iii) monitoring blood glucose, (iv) taking medication, (v) problem solving, (vi) reducing risks and (vii) healthy coping

group-based classes and invited a family member to enhance the self-efficacy of the patients and improve family support

Educational materials included a protocol and a package of patient materials including (i) forms for assessment, goal setting, blood glucose monitoring and ‘pros and cons’ exercise; (ii) information brochures and quick reference pamphlets on diabetes care and (iii) patient’s HbA1c test results letter

Lifestyle and anthropometric outcomes: secondary outcomes of diabetes Self-Management

A total of eleven studies reported anthropometric outcomes [16, 17, 23–25, 31, 32, 35, 37–39], out of which ten reported body mass index (BMI), three reported on weight [16, 17, 31] and two on waist circumference [23, 35]. Meanwhile, only one study reported on muscle, fat, and water mass (Table 2) [16]. Similarly, only AlHaqwi et al. reported on obesity prevalence [38]. Out of eleven studies, 7 consisted of RCTs [16, 17, 24, 25, 32, 35, 39] while 3 were quasi-experimental [23, 31], one was a pre-post design [38], and one was an experimental study [37]. Only six studies showed significant improvements in BMI with varying effect sizes (from small to large) following interventions using TPE constructs [23, 25, 31, 35, 37, 39]. The majority of the articles reported a small effect size for the changes in BMI in the intervention groups following the educational intervention (n = 8) [16, 24, 25, 31, 32, 37–39], while only two reported large and significant effect sizes and changes respectively [16, 23–25, 31, 32, 35, 37–39]. Half of the studies showing a small effect size portrayed non-significant results for BMI changes (n = 4) [16, 24, 32, 38], two did not report on the significance level [31, 39] and two showed significant changes [25, 37]. As for weight, all reported data showed small effect sizes, with two revealing insignificant changes in weight [16, 17] and one article not reporting the level of significance [31]. On the other hand, both Kaveh et al. and Farag Mohamed et al. reported a significantly large effect size for the changes in waist circumference following the educational interventions in the intervention groups [23, 35]. In addition to significant reductions in waist circumference (Cohen’s d = 3.639, p < 0.001, large effect size), Kaveh et al. (2022) showed reductions in BMI (Cohen’s d = 2.078, p < 0.001, large effect size) [23]. Similarly, Farag Mohamed et al. (2021) also showed significant reductions in BMI and waist circumference with large effect sizes (Cohen’s d = 1.67, p = 0.001; Cohen’s d = 3.51, p < 0.001, respectively)(Table 2) [35].

Patients were divided into 2 groups (n = 35):

I1 (n = 18): went through a six-monthsnth behavioral lifestyle program composed of 8 sessions

Intervention group (n = 18) underwent a six-months lifestyle programme composed of 8 sessions, 4 individual sessions and 4 telephone calls.

In this group weight loss and physical activity targets were set, and calories specific diets were given to participants.

The intervention group received structured educational materials developed specifically for the Emirati community regarding sources of carbohydrates and dietary fiber, physical activity, eating out, and healthy cooking methods

Control group (n = 17): 3 individual sessions scheduled at baseline, 1 month and 6 months. These patients received general dietary guideline handouts and calorie-based diets, were provided goals for lifestyle habits and were encouraged to employ self-monitoring.

Women were divided into 2 groups:

I1 included two face-to-face individualized dietary consultations and two telephonic counseling sessions by a licensed dietitian. At recruitment, the first face-to-face consultation occurred and the second was 12 weeks later, while telephone follow-ups were conducted between the two face-to-face.

Participants were provided with guidelines on weight gain, dietary counseling, physical activity counseling, teaching material and follow up tools.

BMI body mass index C control I intervention TPE therapeutic patient education

A total of four studies reported dietary intake outcomes following interventions using TPE constructs (Table 2) [16, 17, 28, 37]. Tori et al. revealed significant improvements in all assessment food groups with a small effect size of the intervention in the groups receiving the educational interventions [28]. In contrast, Abdi et al. found that all assessed food groups significantly improved after the TPE with small effect sizes, except for vegetables, legumes, and other carbohydrates [16]. While some studies showed minor improvements, such as significant reductions in carbohydrate intake with varying effect sizes (Cohen’s d= −0.52, medium effect size, p = 0.00; and Cohen’s d= −0.01, small effect size, p < 0.001) [16, 28], however, there was no clear, consistent improvement in dietary intake across all studies. (Table 2).

Lastly, a total of four studies reported on physical activity outcomes following TPE interventions (Table 2) [16, 17, 35, 37]. The results do not show a clear improvement in minutes of physical activity performed across articles. However, one study showed a significant decrease in the number of participants who reported not having obstacles in undertaking physical activity (Cohen’s h = 0.73, large effect size, p < 0.001) [37]. Given the small number of articles reporting on physical activity and the heterogeneity of the outcome measures used, the impact of TPE intervention in the EMR seems to be inconclusive (Table 2).

Biological outcomes: secondary outcomes of diabetes Self-Management

Sixteen articles revealed different biological measures as assessors of the effect of educational interventions with TPE constructs on the self-management of diabetes [16, 17, 19, 20, 23–25, 27–29, 31–33, 35, 38, 39]. The studies were also of a varied design with ten RCTs [16, 17, 20, 24, 25, 28, 29, 31, 35, 39], five quasi-experimental studies [19, 23, 27, 32, 33], and 1 pre-post design study [38]. The articles reported a variety of biological outcomes, including glycemic, blood pressure, and blood lipid measures. The reported biological measure included HbA1c [16, 19, 20, 23–25, 27–29, 31, 32, 35, 38, 39], fasting blood glucose [17, 23, 25, 28, 29, 33, 35, 38, 39], post-prandial blood glucose [17], low-density lipoproteins (LDL) [16, 25, 31, 35, 38, 39], high-density lipoproteins (HDL) [16, 25, 35, 38, 39], systolic blood pressure (SBP) [16, 23, 35, 38, 39], diastolic blood pressure (DBP) [16, 23, 35, 38, 39], triglycerdies [16, 25, 31, 35, 38, 39], and total cholesterol [16, 25, 35, 38, 39]. A total of 14 articles reported changes in HbA1c, and the effect size of the educational intervention ranged from small to large effect sizes. An equal number of articles reported small (n = 5) and large effect sizes (n = 5), while only four articles reported medium effect sizes. All studies reporting a large effect size for HbA1c revealed a significant difference in the glycated hemoglobin after the intervention [19, 23, 27, 35, 38]. Among all the articles that reported a small effect size [16, 24, 25, 28, 32], only one revealed a non-significant difference in HbA1c following the educational intervention [25]. Similarly, studies reporting a medium effect size all reported significant changes in HbA1c [20, 29, 31, 39] except for one article that did not report on the levels of the significance of HbA1c secondary to the intervention [39]. A total of eight articles reported on fasting blood glucose before and after the educational intervention, three of which reported significant changes in blood glucose and small effect sizes [25, 28, 29] 2 medium effect sizes [38, 39], and 3 large effect sizes [23, 33, 35]. Two studies that revealed a large effect size showed significant changes in fasting blood glucose after the intervention [23, 35] and only one article did not report on the significance level of the change [33]. Similarly, one study reporting a medium effect size did not present the level of significance of the change [39] while the other showed significant changes [38]. For example, Kaveh et al. reported significant changes and large effect sizes in HbA1C and fasting blood glucose after the educational intervention had been implemented (Cohen’s d = 12.47, p < 0.001; Cohen’s d = 9.33, p < 0.001, respectively). Data reporting on blood pressure levels showed that two articles identified large and significant effect sizes of changes in SBP and DBP [23, 35]. On the other hand, two studies revealed a small effect size of the intervention, with one reporting non-significant results [16, 39] and the other lacking the level of significance for both SBP and DBP [39]. AlHaqwi et al. revealed no effect of the intervention on blood pressure measurements, with significant results for SBP but not DBP [38]. Data reporting on blood lipids also presented varying effect sizes, with the majority of the results showing small effect sizes of the intervention on the measurements. A total of five and six articles identified changes in total cholesterol [16, 25, 35, 38, 39] and triglycerides [16, 25, 31, 35, 38, 39] respectively. For both blood lipid measures, only two articles showed medium effect sizes [35, 39] with only Farag Mohamed et al. presenting significant changes in total cholesterol and triglycerides following the educational interventions [35]. The remaining studies revealed a small effect size, with only AlHaqwi et al. reporting a small yet significant change in total cholesterol and triglycerides [38]. Studies reporting on HDL identified a non-significant and the absence of effect of the educational intervention in two studies [35, 38]. Likewise, two studies showed a small yet non-significant effect size for changes in HDL. On the other hand, AlHaqwi et al. revealed a small effect size with significant changes in LDL. The three other studies reporting a small effect size for LDL secondary to the educational intervention were either non-significant (n = 2) [16, 25] or failed to report the level of significance [31]. While Mohamed et al. identified a medium effect size for changes in both HDL and LDL, the change was not significant [39]. Farag Mohamed et al. also reported two effect sizes for LDL depending on the phase of the cross-over RCT, revealing either a small or a large significant effect size [35].

Patients were divided into 2 groups:

Intervention group: went through a six-month behavioral lifestyle program composed of 8 sessions

Control group: received standard care

Intervention group underwent a six-month lifestyle programme composed of 8 sessions, 4 individual sessions and 4 telephone calls.

In this group weight loss and physical activity targets were set, and calories specific diets were given to participants.

The intervention group received structured educational materials developed specifically for the Emirati community regarding sources of carbohydrates and dietary fiber, physical activity, eating out, and healthy cooking methods

Control group: 3 individual sessions scheduled at baseline, 1 month and 6 months. These patients received general dietary guideline handouts and calorie-based diets, were provided goals for lifestyle habits and were encouraged to employ self-monitoring.

Women were divided into 2 groups:

I1: Lifetsyle intervention: a 12-week, moderate-intensity lifestyle intervention with individualized counseling on a diet, physical activity, and behavior change

C: Usual care: received usual anenatal care (general advice regarding lifestyle changes)

LI included two face-to-face individualized dietary consultations and two telephonic counseling sessions by a licensed dietitian. At recruitment, the first face-to-face consultation occurred and the second was 12weeks later, while telephone follow-ups were conducted between the two face-to-face.

Participants were provided with guidelines on weight gain, dietary counseling, physical activity counseling, teaching material and follow up tools.

C control, I Intervention

Risk of bias in the studies reporting TPE interventions in the EMR

All fourteen RCTs were assessed for the risk of bias using the RoB2 tool for individual parallel RCTs or crossover trials [14]. The assessment resulted in three low-risk-of-bias trials and 4 high-risk-of-bias trials. A total of 7 RCTs showed some concerns with either one or more of the domains of the RoB tool. The JBI tool was used for the assessment of the quasi-experimental, pre-post, and experimental studies [15]. The assessment revealed a low risk for 3 studies, and the rest showed some concerns regarding their risk of bias. The figures and tables for the risk assessment of each article using the different tools are presented in the additional material (see Additional File 5 for the results of the RoB2 tool and Additional File 6 for the results of the JBI tool).

Discussion

This systematic review identified 48 articles assessing the impact of educational interventions on diabetes self-management in the EMR, with 24 articles utilizing at least one TPE component or two TPE elements of the patient competency framework. Our analysis revealed that the articles used multiple measures to assess the impact and effectiveness of educational interventions on diabetes self-management. These measures were reflected as outcomes in the results and included self-care outcomes, biological, anthropometric, and lifestyle outcomes.

Our results showed that self-care was one of the most reported assessors for the educational interventions on self-management of diabetes (n = 18). Self-care practices have been shown to improve diabetes through reducing blood sugar and complications of the disease, as well as increasing the life expectancy of individuals with diabetes mellitus [40, 41]. Reduction in diabetes complications, progression, and burden of the disease has also been attributed to increased adherence to self-care [40, 41]. The improvement of at least one component of the self-care adherence measures in the results of this review reflects the importance of integrating TPE elements and constructs into the educational interventions of diabetes to effectively manage the disease in the long term. Despite continuous efforts to maximize self-care in individuals with diabetes, various barriers and factors influence continual adherence to self-care [42]. It is therefore important for health care providers to assess individual knowledge, skills, and emotional factors combined with self-efficacy and the different areas of self-care [42]. This is addressed by TPE, particularly as part of the patient competency profile; thus, there is a pressing need to shift from traditional educational approaches to more people-centered care, i.e., TPE [6]. This fosters a therapeutic alliance with individuals living with diabetes and recognizes them as partners in their own care plan. Therefore, this emphasizes the need for shared decision-making, goal setting, and action planning to empower individuals to effectively manage their condition [6]. This shift is critical for the integration of TPE into the healthcare systems and accurately assessing its effectiveness in improving diabetes-related health outcomes, such as the various self-management outcomes that were assessed in this review.

Biological outcomes such as glycated hemoglobin (HbA1c), fasting blood glucose, blood pressure, and lipid profiles are widely used indicators of diabetes management and reflect the effectiveness of self-care interventions. In our results, we observed considerable variability in effect sizes across biological outcomes, including glycated hemoglobin (HbA1c), fasting blood glucose, blood pressure, and blood lipid measures. This pattern aligns with Correia et al., who similarly reported variations of effect sizes for HbA1c largely influenced by the delivery agent of the intervention, despite significant reductions in glycated hemoglobin [43]. Like our results, reductions in glycated hemoglobin were apparent in the majority of reviews following the implementation of educational interventions in individuals with diabetes [44–48]. TPE interventions have also been shown to effectively improve biological outcomes in chronic diseases in addition to treatment adherence and psychological health of individuals [49]. The variability in effect sizes for biological measures observed in this review highlights the importance of tailoring educational interventions to individual needs, which aligns with key elements of TPE. Anthropometric outcomes were mostly reported on body weight and BMI in our findings. This heterogeneity has been similarly reported by Correia et al., who attributed these differences to both delivery agents and modalities of intervention [50]. Similarly, another review reporting on the effect of TPE interventions on various outcomes in individuals with diabetes revealed significant improvements in BMI among individuals who received TPE, further supporting the potential of TPE to positively influence anthropometric health indicators [51].

The variability in these outcomes across studies underscores the complex interplay between intervention design, delivery, and participant characteristics, highlighting the need for a more personalized approach to educational interventions for diabetes management. Furthermore, the variability in effect sizes may be attributed to differences in intervention intensity, duration, and fidelity of delivery. Additionally, the extent of engagement and concordance with the interventions likely played a role, yet these aspects were often underreported or not assessed. The differences in the healthcare system in the region may have also influenced the implementation of TPE interventions, further contributing to this variability.

Our results revealed that a total of 20 articles addressed at least two components of the patient competency framework, with knowledge and confidence/self-efficacy being the most reported. Studies that incorporated these components reported significant improvements in self-care and biological outcomes, thus emphasizing their effectiveness in supporting diabetes self-management. Moreover, the presence of two or more TPE constructs was consistently observed in interventions with medium to large effect sizes. These results reinforce the multidimensional nature of effective patient education.

In terms of TPE elements, goal setting was the most reported [16, 17, 20, 23, 24, 31, 33, 39] with fewer studies reporting on shared decision-making and action planning as part of their educational intervention for diabetes [21, 27, 29, 31, 33, 39]. Our data also identified only one article that employed all three constructs of TPE in their educational intervention for diabetes mellitus [31]. This indicates a limited application of the TPE wheel in diabetes care despite its potential to enhance self-management. This systematic review presented various studies utilizing TPE elements in EMR; however, published research on TPE in the region remains fragmented and localized to a limited number of countries. The findings indicate that some RCTs yielded significant improvements when structured effectively with comprehensive TPE components [35, 39]; this is, however, not observed across all RCTs. Varying effect sizes were presented across different study designs for almost all outcomes being investigated.

The results reported a substantial diversity of healthcare providers implementing educational interventions. Likewise, Waqas et al. observed that the use of TPE in severe mental illnesses also involved multiple healthcare providers, with multidisciplinary teams utilized in certain cases [52]. Other reviews of TPE interventions in chronic diseases, overweight and obesity, and diabetes revealed that interventions were mostly delivered by allied healthcare providers, followed by multidisciplinary teams [49, 50].

Our systematic review further demonstrated that TPE interventions were delivered through various modalities, with workshops being the most common. In contrast, Waqas et al. (2024) reported that the primary modality for delivering TPE in mental health settings was interactive presentations rather than didactic approaches (n = 40), with only 2 interventions delivered digitally [52]. Similarly, Correia et al. reported that most of the TPE interventions in chronic diseases, overweight and obesity, and diabetes were delivered through face-to-face groups [49, 50]. Individualized TPE interventions were also reported among the reviews, with lower rates compared to group interventions [49, 50, 52]. However, when utilizing individualized face-to-face intervention, it is essential to consider the addition of ongoing support to enhance the overall management of diabetes [43]. Digitalized TPE interventions were also applied in the context of overweight, obesity, and diabetes [50]. Our results also revealed that physicians delivered educational interventions less frequently compared to other allied healthcare providers. Thus, the role that healthcare providers, particularly physicians, play in increasing the self-care of individuals with diabetes should be augmented to increase individual adherence to self-care and reduce diabetes progression [40]. Therefore, our findings, along with the evidence in the literature, demonstrate the potential of a wide range of healthcare providers to implement TPE. Such adaptability supports the practical application of TPE in diverse multiple settings, making it a valuable approach for improving diabetes management.

To our knowledge, this study is the first to assess the effects of TPE interventions on diabetes self-management outcomes in the EMR. Additionally, this review aimed to capture interventions that truly reflected TPE rather than traditional education by excluding articles that did not address at least one TPE element or two combinations of the components of the patient competency profile. This approach ensures a more targeted and concrete evaluation of TPE interventions in the EMR, distinguishing it from other published reviews. With multiple EMR countries classified under states of emergency, including Afghanistan, Libya, Yemen, Syria, Somalia, and the Occupied Palestinian Territories [53] the results of this systematic review will offer an insight into the effectiveness of TPE in a region facing unique healthcare challenges. This review also responds to and bridges the gap of the deficiency in research articles on TPE in low- and middle-income countries [54]. Furthermore, the inclusion of a broad range of outcomes, self-care, biological, lifestyle, and anthropometric measures provides a comprehensive understanding of how TPE impacts various angles of diabetes management. However, this systematic review has notable limitations. To begin with, most articles had one TPE component or two TPE elements of the patient competency framework rather than a full application of TPE. This prevented our results from stating the effectiveness of TPE interventions with confidence. Therefore, future research should aim to design studies that implement TPE based on the TPE wheel proposed by the WHO guide [6] so that the impact of TPE interventions can be better reported across the literature. The variability in study designs and the heterogeneity of some of the reported outcomes made it challenging to perform a meta-analysis, limiting the ability to assess the overall impact of TPE quantitatively. Another limitation applies to the included studies that delivered interventions to the control groups. People living with diabetes entering the medical system cannot ignore any information or any learning process. Thus, it is almost impossible to avoid giving any information to the control groups. As discussed previously in the literature and for a long time [55], some RCTs have an approach bias towards the control group. For instance, some of them have offered both the control and intervention groups an initial educational intervention that should be considered when interpreting the impact of TPE on chronic disease outcomes. Thus, the true benefit of the TPE intervention may be difficult to detect when the control group receives advice beyond standard care.

Additionally, most of the studies had short follow-up periods, making it difficult to determine the long-term effects of TPE on diabetes outcomes. The results of this systematic review cannot be generalized to all types of diabetes mellitus, as most studies reported the effectiveness of TPE intervention on T2DM in the adult population.

Conclusion

This systematic review demonstrates the potential of TPE interventions in the EMR to improve diabetes self-management outcomes, particularly self-care, biological, lifestyle, and anthropometric outcomes. The results of this review demonstrate that TPE interventions improve self-care behaviors, such as general or specific diet adherence, blood glucose monitoring, foot care, and medication adherence, with studies reporting effect sizes ranging from small to large for various activities. Similarly, varying results were reported for anthropometric and biological outcomes, with most studies revealing small effect sizes for changes in BMI, weight, and blood lipid measures in the intervention groups following TPE. On the other hand, few studies reported large and significant improvements in waist circumference, HbA1c, and fasting blood glucose. The findings reveal that TPE interventions encompassing components of the patient competency framework and TPE constructs positively impact measured outcomes of diabetes mellitus. However, the limited number of studies reporting on all components of the TPE wheel, variability in effect sizes, limited evidence on dietary and physical activity outcomes, and limited application of certain TPE components emphasize the need for more structured and context-specific TPE interventions relevant to the EMR and the NCDs that are mostly prevalent in the region, primarily diabetes. These results reveal the importance of expanding the implementation of TPE to improve diabetes management and clinical and health outcomes in individuals with diabetes mellitus.
